8.  Conclusion

We provide a method for estimating SDPD models with unknown spatial weights using series approximations, while maintaining ease of implementation.
We allow the spatial weights in the outcome, lagged-outcome, and disturbance channels to be unknown functions of exogenous distance measures and cover both SAR and MESS specifications. Using sieve-based GMMEs, we construct an OGMME that is robust to unknown heteroskedasticity and a more efficient feasible BGMME via both linear and quadratic moments. This estimation approach can be directly extended to time-varying exogenous distances without any technical difficulties. Testing and selecting models between SAR and MESS matrix remain an area for future research.

Appendix A
Notations and Definitions

We first collect all notations in our paper.
Although some of them are already defined in the main text we believe it will be useful to state all notations at this point.

Notation A1
.

(i)
For the sieve approximation, define
\ell_{n}=\max_{1\leq k\leq 3}\ell_{k}
and
\underline{\varsigma}=\min_{1\leq k\leq 3}\varsigma_{k}
.

(ii) Define
H=\{h_{ij}\}
as the matrix whose
(i,j)
-th entry is
h_{ij}
.
Then, for
k=1,2,3
and
j=1,...,\ell_{k}
, we assume
g_{k}(d)=\sum_{p_{k}=1}^{\ell_{k}}\lambda_{p_{k}}\phi_{kp_{k}}(d)+\delta_{k}(d)
, where
\phi_{kp_{k}}(\cdot)
are basis functions and
\delta_{k}(d)
are suitably decaying approximation errors.
We then denote the sieve approximation and related matrices as
\xi_{k}(d)=\sum_{p_{k}=1}^{\ell_{k}}\lambda_{p_{k}}\phi_{kp_{k}}(d)
,
\Xi_{k}=\{\xi_{k}(d_{ij})\}
, and
\varPhi_{kp_{k}}=\{\phi_{kp_{k}}(d_{ij})\}
. Defining
\Delta_{k}=\{\delta_{k}(d_{ij})\}
, we obtain the decomposition
B_{k}=S_{k}+R_{k}
, where
S_{k}
depends on the sieve matrix
\Xi_{k}
and
R_{k}
collects the approximation error matrix. Specifically, for the SAR,
S_{1}=I_{n}-\Xi_{1},S_{2}=\Xi_{2},S_{3}=I_{n}-\Xi_{3}
with
R_{1}=-\Delta_{1},R_{2}=\Delta_{2},R_{3}=-\Delta_{3}
; for the MESS,
S_{k}=e^{\Xi_{k}}
and
R_{k}=S_{k}(e^{\Delta_{k}}-I_{n})
.

(iii) For a set of matrices
M_{j}
,
j=1,...,p
, the following equation represents a specific matrix operation:

M_{1}[M_{2},M_{3},...,M_{p-1}]M_{p}=[M_{1}M_{2}M_{p},M_{1}M_{3}M_{p},...,M_{1}M_{p-1}M_{p}]

where the column-dimension in
M_{m}
is equal to the row-dimension in
M_{1}
, and the row-dimension in
M_{m}
is equal to the column-dimension in
M_{p}
, for
m=2,...,p-1
.

(iv) For the FOD transformation,
Let
[F_{T,T-1},\frac{l_{T}}{\sqrt{T}}]
be the orthogonal matrix of
J_{T}=I_{T}-\frac{1}{T}l_{T}l_{T}^{\prime}
, where
l_{T}
is the
T\times 1
vector of ones, and then, the
n\times T
matrix
\left[H_{1},H_{2},\cdots,H_{T}\right]
can be transformed into the
n(T-1)
matrix
\left[H_{1}^{*},H_{2}^{*},\cdots,H_{T-1}^{*}\right]=\left[H_{1},H_{2},\cdots,H_{T}\right]F_{T,T-1}
. Also, the
n(T-1)
matrix
[H_{0}^{(*,-1)},H_{1}^{(*,-1)},\cdots,H_{T-2}^{(*,-1)}]=[H_{0},H_{1},\cdots,H_{T-1}]F_{T,T-1}
. For example,
Y_{t}^{*}=h_{Tt}(Y_{t}-\sum_{h=t+1}^{T}Y_{h})
and
Y_{t-1}^{(*,-1)}=h_{Tt}(Y_{t-1}-\sum_{h=t}^{T-1}Y_{h})
depend on current and future variables, but not on the past ones, where
h_{Tt}=\sqrt{\frac{T-t}{T-t+1}}
.

(v) For the stacked matrix form,
\mathbf{H}_{N}^{*}=(H_{1}^{*\prime},...,H_{T-1}^{*\prime})^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{H}_{N}^{(*,-1)}=(H_{0}^{(*,-1)\prime},...,H_{T-2}^{(*,-1)\prime})^{\prime}
for any
n\times\ell_{h}
matrices
H_{t}
and
H_{t-1}
with full columns.
For example,
\mathbf{Y}_{N}^{*}=(Y_{1}^{*\prime},...,Y_{T-1}^{*\prime})
,
\mathbf{Y}_{N}^{(*,-1)}=(Y_{0}^{(*,-1)\prime},...,Y_{T-2}^{(*,-1)\prime})
and
\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*}=(E_{1}^{*\prime},...,E_{T-1}^{*\prime})
.
For
k=1,2,3
and
j=1,..,\ell_{k}
,
\mathbf{S}_{kN}=I_{T-1}\otimes S_{k}
,
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{kj,N}=I_{T-1}\otimes\varPhi_{kj}
, where
\varPhi_{kj}
and
S_{k}
are defined as Notation
A1
(ii). Thus,

\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{*}=[\mathbf{Y}_{N}^{(*,-1)},\mathbf{X}_{N}^{*}]
, and

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{T-1}^{*}=(\alpha_{0}^{*},...,\alpha_{T-1}^{*})^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
\varPhi_{k}=[\varPhi_{k1},\ldots,\varPhi_{kj}]
,
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{Nk}=[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{k1,N},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{kj,N}]
, and
\mathbf{L}_{N}^{*}=\mathbf{S}_{3N}\left[\mathbf{Z}_{N}^{*},\tfrac{\partial\mathbf{S}_{1N}}{\lambda_{1}}\mathbf{Y}_{N}^{*},\tfrac{\partial\mathbf{S}_{2N}}{\lambda_{2}}\mathbf{Y}_{N}^{(*,-1)},\mathbf{0}_{N\times\ell_{3}}\right]
with

\tfrac{\partial\mathbf{S}_{kN}}{\partial\lambda_{k}}=\left[\tfrac{\partial\mathbf{S}_{kN}}{\partial\lambda_{k1}},\cdots,\tfrac{\partial\mathbf{S}_{kN}}{\partial\lambda_{kj}}\right]
.

(vi)
Denote
N=n(T-1)
and
\theta=(\pi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})^{\prime}
, where
\pi=(\gamma,\beta^{\prime})^{\prime}
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime},\lambda_{3}^{\prime})^{\prime}
with
\lambda_{k}=(\lambda_{k1},...,\lambda_{kp_{k}})^{\prime}
for
k=1,2,3
. Denote
\hat{g}_{k}(d)=\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}^{\prime}(d)\hat{\lambda}_{k}
, where
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{k}(d)=[\phi_{k1}(d),...,\phi_{k\ell_{k}}(d)]
and
\hat{\lambda}_{k}
is an estimator of
\lambda_{k}
. For the dimension,

\dim(\theta)=\ell_{\theta}
,
\dim(\pi)=\ell_{\pi}
,
\dim(\lambda)=\ell_{\lambda}
,
\dim(m_{N}^{\texttt{quad}}(\theta))=\ell_{p}
,
\dim(m_{N}^{\texttt{line}}(\theta))=\ell_{q}
,
\dim(m_{N}(\theta))=\ell_{m}=\ell_{p}+\ell_{q}
.
.

We then introduce the following definitions.

Definition A1
.

(i) For any real time-varying
n\times n
random matrix
H_{t}
, we say that
H_{t}
has Property UB if

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\|H_{t}\|_{\mathrm{rc}}

\displaystyle=O_{p}(1),\ \sup_{t}\|H_{t}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O_{p}(1),\ \text{and}\ \sup_{t}\|H_{t}\|=O_{p}(\sqrt{n}),

or the non-stochastic version

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\|H_{t}\|_{\mathrm{rc}}

\displaystyle=O(1),\ \sup_{t}\|H_{t}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O(1),\ \text{and}\ \sup_{t}\|H_{t}\|=O(\sqrt{n})

(ii) For any real time-varying
n\times n
random matrix
H_{t}
, we say that
H_{t}
has Property
O_{p}(h^{j})
if

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t}\|H_{t}\|_{\mathrm{rc}}

\displaystyle=O_{p}(h^{j}),\ \sup\limits_{t}\|H_{t}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O_{p}(h^{j}),\ \text{and}\ \sup\limits_{t}\|H_{t}\|=O_{p}((\sqrt{n}h)^{j}),

or the non-stochastic version
O(h^{j})

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t}\|H_{t}\|_{\mathrm{rc}}

\displaystyle=O(h^{j}),\ \sup\limits_{t}\|H_{t}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O(h^{j}),\ \text{and}\ \sup\limits_{t}\|H_{t}\|=O((\sqrt{n}h)^{j})

with
h\to 0
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sqrt{n}h\to 0
for some positive integer
j
.

Definition A2
.

For any real square matrix (or random matrix)
H_{N}
, where
N=n(T-1)
, we say that
H_{N}
has Property SP if

\displaystyle\sup_{N}\|H_{N}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mu_{\max}(H_{N}^{\prime}H_{N})=O_{p}(1),\ \text{and}\ \bigl(\sup_{N}\mu_{\min}(H_{N}^{\prime}H_{N})\bigr)^{-1}=O_{p}(1),

or the non-stochastic version

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\|H_{N}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mu_{\max}(H_{N}^{\prime}H_{N})<\infty,\ \text{and}\ \lim_{N\to\infty}\mu_{\min}(H_{N}^{\prime}H_{N})>0.

Definition
A1
introduces the UB and convergence to zero for certain
n\times n
random and nonrandom matrices in each
t
.
For example, according to Lemma
S2
(ii),
S_{k}
’s satisfy
A1
and
R_{k}
’s satisfy
A1

O_{p}(\ell_{n})
.
Definition
A2
captures boundedness and non-multicollinearity properties of random or fixed matrices of growing dimension.

Appendix B
Regularity Assumptions

Assumption B1
.

The
\epsilon_{it}
are independent with zero mean and finite variances that are bounded away from zero (denoted by
\sigma^{2}
,
\sigma_{i}^{2}
, or
\sigma_{t}^{2}
), as discussed in
3.2
,
3.2
and
3.2
, respectively.
Furthermore,
\operatorname{E}|\epsilon_{it}|^{4+\delta_{\epsilon}}\leq C_{\epsilon}
for some constant
C_{\epsilon}
and
\delta_{\epsilon}>0
.
A1
A1

A1

If
0<\delta_{\epsilon}<1
, where
\delta_{\epsilon}
is in Assumption
B1
, then we need
\delta>\frac{p-2}{p+4}
to guarantee the CLT. Discussions are in footnote
A6
. Moreover, Assumption
1
is required for the joint limit theory where
(n,T)\to\infty
. For the fixed
T
case in Remark
3
, this rate condition is not required.

Assumption B2
.

For
k=1,2,3
and
l=1,2,\cdots,\ell_{k}
:

(i)
\|G_{k}\|_{\mathrm{rc}}
and
\|\Upsilon\|_{\mathrm{rc}}
are uniformly bounded in
n
, and
\rho(A)<1
, where
A=B_{1}^{-1}(\gamma I_{n}+B_{2})
,
\Upsilon=\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{abs}(A^{h})
,
[\mathrm{abs}(A)]_{ij}=|A_{ij}|
and
A_{ij}
is the
(i,j)
-th element of
A
. Moreover, the initial condition
Y_{0}
is exogenously given.

(ii) The basis functions
\phi_{kl}(\cdot)
satisfy

\sup_{i,k,l}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\phi_{kl}(d_{ij})|<\infty
,

\sup_{j,k,l}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\phi_{kl}(d_{ij})|<\infty
.

(iii) For some fixed
\tau>1
,
\sup_{l}|\lambda_{l}l^{\tau}|<\infty
.

(iii’) The
(i,j)
th element of the approximation error matrix
\Delta_{kl}
, defined as

\delta_{k}(d_{ij})=\sum_{l=\ell_{k}+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}\phi_{kl}(d_{ij})
, satisfying
|\delta_{k}(d_{ij})|=O(l^{-\varsigma_{k}})
for some
\varsigma_{k}>2
.

Assumption
B1
provides regularity assumptions for the idiosyncratic term, and
\|\Sigma_{t}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O(1)
and
\|\Sigma_{t}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O(1)
since
\tfrac{1}{\mu_{\min}(\Sigma_{t})}=O(1)
. In particular, we require the existence of fourth moments, thereby relaxing the condition in
Yang et al. 2025
and
Gupta et al. 2025
, which assumes the existence of eighth moments.
Assumption
B2
imposes the structures of unknown spatial weights matrices
G_{k}
’s and approximation tail matrices
\Delta_{k}
’s.
The condition of (i) is parallel to assumptions on spatial weights in parametric SDPD models, see, e.g.
Yu et al. 2008
;
Lee and Yu 2014
.
A2
A2

A2

One may also consider settings in which
G_{k}
contains “stars”, so that the column sums grow with
n
, see, e.g.
Pesaran and Yang 2021
;
Lee et al. 2022
;
Zhang et al. 2025
. We leave this extension for future research.

\|G_{k}\|_{\mathrm{rc}}
requires that
g_{k}(d_{ij})
either has a compact support or approaches zero fast enough for large
d_{ij}
values, and that a growing sample increases the domain, not the density of
d_{ij}
. Such assumptions are also discussed in
Sun 2016
and
Chen et al. 2025
. Alternatively,
Pinkse et al. 2002
assume that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[d_{ij}\in D]<\infty
for any fixed domain set
D
, where
\mathbf{1}[\cdot]
is the indicator function. This condition is sufficient to ensure
\|G_{k}\|_{\mathrm{rc}}<\infty
the when
g_{k}(\cdot)
has bounded support
D
.
\rho(A)<1
indicates that the dynamic system (
1
) is stable, ruling out unit root or related issues.

\|\Upsilon\|_{\mathrm{rc}}<\infty
combines the absolute summability condition and the UB condition of the power series of
A
. We consider the initial condition
Y_{0}
is exogenously given, which does not need to be specified in the estimation or asymptotic analysis.

A3
A3

A3

For the finite
T
case, one may specify the initial condition that is endogenous. Such a specification is beyond the scope of this paper and deserves further study.

(ii) implies that each approximation matrix
\varPhi_{kl}=\{\phi_{kl}(d_{ij})\}
is UB in both row and column sums. Also, it requires that there are only a finite number of neighbors for each
i
such that
\phi_{kl}(d_{ij})\neq 0
in the limit. This means
\phi_{kl}(d_{ij})
declines fast enough with growing
d_{ij}
, and that observations spread out in an increasing domain.
(iii) is a ‘smoothness condition’, and the polynomial expansion can be used by this condition, as discussed in
Pinkse et al. 2002
.
By (iii), we can deduce that
|\delta_{k}(d_{ij})|=O_{p}(l^{-\varsigma_{k}})=o_{p}(1)
. (iii’) corresponds to a more general setting. In particular, when
g_{k}(d_{ij})
is supported on an unbounded domain, such as
[0,\infty)
or
(-\infty,\infty)
, the basis function
\{\phi_{kl}(\cdot)\}
can be chosen as normalized Laguerre (or Hermite) orthonormal polynomials, following
Sun 2016
.

Assumption B3
.

\{x_{it}\}
,
\{c_{i}\}
and
\{\alpha_{t}\}
are uniformly bounded constants.

Assumption
B3
introduces the UB condition of
X_{t}
and FE, which can also be found in
Lee and Yu 2014
and
Su et al. 2023
. We can also assume
\sup_{1\leq l\leq\ell_{x}}\operatorname{E}|x_{lit}|^{2}\leq C_{x}
for some constant
C_{x}
. By Assumptions
B1
,
B2
(i) and
B3
, we can deduce that
\|Y_{t}\|=O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
and
\|Y_{t-1}\|=O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
by the backward substitution.

Assumption B4

(IV matrix)
.

(i) The typical elements of
Q_{t}
, denoted by
q_{ijt}
for
i=1,\cdots,n
and
j=1,\cdots,\ell_{q}
, satisfy
\operatorname{E}|q_{ijt}|^{2}\leq C_{q}
with some constant
C_{q}
. Furthermore,
\operatorname{E}\bigl(\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}Q_{t}^{\prime}E_{t}^{*}\big|\mathcal{I}_{t-1}\bigr)=0
, where
\mathcal{I}_{t-1}
denotes the
\sigma
-field spanned
by
(Y_{0},\cdots,Y_{t-1})
, conditional on
(X_{1},\cdots,X_{T},\mathbf{c}_{n},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{T}
).

(ii) For each
j=1,...,\ell_{p}
,
P_{jt}
satisfies
A1
.

(iii)
\lim\limits_{n,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{N}
,
\lim\limits_{n,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}J_{N}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}J_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{N}
, and
\lim\limits_{n,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{J}({\bm{\Sigma}_{N}})\mathbf{Q}_{N}
satisfy
A2
.

(iv)
\ell_{q}\sim\ell_{n}
and
\ell_{p}\sim\ell_{n}
.

Assumption
B4
collects the regularity conditions on the instrumental variables.
Condition (i) imposes the standard IV requirements used in SDPD models (see, e.g.
Lee and Yu 2014
).
Condition (ii) requires a UB property for the IV matrices entering the quadratic moment conditions, which in turn guarantees that
\varPhi_{kl}
is well-defined. Importantly, we do not impose any additional high-level restrictions on the instruments, such as
\operatorname{tr}(J_{n}P_{jt})=0
(as in
Lee and Yu 2014
) or
\mathtt{Diag}(P_{jt})=0
(as in
Lin and Lee 2010
).
Condition (iii) ensures the existence of the asymptotic covariance matrix
\Omega_{N}
, which involves the inverse of
\lim\limits_{n,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}J_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{N}
in the 2SLS approch, the inverse of
\lim\limits_{n,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}J_{N}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}J_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{N}
in the OGMM approach and the inverse of
\lim\limits_{n,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{J}({\bm{\Sigma}_{N}})\mathbf{\Sigma}_{N}\boldsymbol{J}({\bm{\Sigma}_{N}})\mathbf{Q}_{N}=\lim\limits_{n,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{J}({\bm{\Sigma}_{N}})\mathbf{Q}_{N}
in the BGMM approach.
Finally, condition (iv) restricts the dimension of the moment conditions,
\ell_{m}=\ell_{p}+\ell_{q}\sim\ell_{n}
, so that the number of moments grows at the same rate as the cross-sectional dimension
n
, in line with
Gupta et al. 2025
. Unlike the setting in
Lee and Yu 2014
, where the many-moments problem arises from both spatial and time expansions, in our framework, it stems solely
from the number of instruments,
\ell_{m}
, which increases with the order of the spatial power series used to approximate the unknown spatial weights. To control this growth, we impose explicit rate conditions that ensure the expansion order (and hence
\ell_{m}
) is asymptotically dominated by both the cross-sectional
dimension
n
and the time dimension
T
.

Assumption B5

(Identification)
.

(i)

\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{L}_{N}^{*}
and

\frac{1}{n(T-1)}\mathbf{L}_{N}^{*\prime}\boldsymbol{J}({\bm{\Sigma}_{N}})\mathbf{L}_{N}^{*}
satisfy
A2
, where
\mathbf{L}_{N}^{*}
is defined in Notation
A1
.

(ii)

\Omega_{N}
satisfies
A2
, where
\Omega_{N}
is defined in equation (
6
).

(iii)
D_{N}^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}D_{N}
satisfies
A2
, where
D_{N}
is defined in equation (
C15
).

Assumption
B5
is a regularity condition to ensure the identifiability of
\theta_{0}
. From (iii), we have
\lim_{n,T\to\infty}\|D_{N}^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}D_{N}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O(1)
.

Appendix C
Lemmas and Theorems

C.1  Key lemmas

In this section, we introduce the key lemmas, while the remaining lemmas are collected in the supplementary file.
In the following part, we denote the true estimand as
\theta_{0}=(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},\lambda_{0}^{\prime})^{\prime}
.

Assumption C6
.

Let
\mathbf{a}\sim(0,\mathbf{\Sigma}_{a})
, where
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{a}=\mathtt{Diag}(\sigma_{a1}^{2},\dots,\sigma_{an}^{2})
, be an
n\times 1
random vector with independent entries
a_{i}
. For each entry
a_{i}
:

(i)
\operatorname{E}[a_{i}]=0
.

(ii)
0<\operatorname{E}[|a_{i}|^{k}]\leq C_{k}
for
k=1,2,3,4
.

(iii)
Let
\mathbf{M}
be an
n\times n
projection matrix that satisfies
\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{M})\leq C_{\mathbf{M}}\cdot m
w.p.1 for large
n
and some constant
C_{\mathbf{M}}
.

Lemma C1
.

Abhi
: This is a trivial result…do we really need to state it as a lemma?

Under Assumption
C6
,

\operatorname{E}\|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}\|=O(m).

Proof.

Note that
\operatorname{E}\|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}\|=\operatorname{E}|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}|
since
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}
is a scalar.
Since
M
is a projection matrix,
|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}|=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}
. Conditioning on
\mathbf{M}
,
we have

\operatorname{E}[\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{a}\mid\mathbf{M}]=\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{M}\bm{\Sigma}_{a})\leq C_{2}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{M})\leq C_{2}C_{\mathbf{M}}m=O_{p}(m)
by Assumption
C6
.
Taking expectations and using the tower property, the proof is finished.

\blacksquare

Lemma C2
.

(i) For any time-varying
n\times n
matrix
H_{t}
, denote
H^{\diamond}_{t}
as a transformed version of
H_{t}
, such that
\left[H^{\diamond}_{t}\right]_{ij}=[H_{t}]_{ij}
if
i\neq j
, and

\displaystyle\left[H^{\diamond}_{t}\right]_{ii}=\frac{1}{n-2}\sum_{j\neq i}^{n}\big([H_{t}]_{ij}+[H_{t}]_{ji}\big)-\frac{1}{(n-1)(n-2)}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k\neq j}^{n}[H_{t}]_{jk}.

(C1)

Then,
\mathtt{diagM}\left(J_{n}H^{\diamond}_{t}J_{n}\right)=\mathbf{0}
.

(ii)
In the case of
3.2
, the vector of diagonal elements
\mathbf{h}_{diag}^{\diamond}=([H_{t}]_{11}^{\diamond},\dots,[H_{t}]_{nn}^{\diamond})^{\prime}
is the solution to
C\mathbf{h}_{diag}^{\diamond}=d
, given by
C^{+}d
, where:

C=\begin{pmatrix}1-\frac{2\sigma_{1}^{-2}}{\varsigma}+\frac{\sigma_{1}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}&\frac{\sigma_{2}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\sigma_{n}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}\\
\frac{\sigma_{1}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}&1-\frac{2\sigma_{2}^{-2}}{\varsigma}+\frac{\sigma_{2}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\sigma_{n}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{\sigma_{1}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}&\frac{\sigma_{2}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}&\cdots&1-\frac{2\sigma_{n}^{-2}}{\varsigma}+\frac{\sigma_{n}^{-4}}{\varsigma^{2}}\end{pmatrix},

with
\varsigma=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sigma_{k}^{-2}
. The
i
-th element of the vector
d
is:

d_{i}=\frac{1}{\varsigma\sigma_{i}^{2}}\left(\sum_{j\neq i}\sigma_{j}^{-2}[H_{t}]_{ji}+\sum_{j\neq i}\sigma_{j}^{-2}[H_{t}]_{ij}\right)-\frac{1}{\varsigma^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k\neq j}\sigma_{j}^{-2}\sigma_{k}^{-2}[H_{t}]_{jk}.

In the cases of
3.2
and
3.2
,
\left[H^{\diamond}_{t}\right]_{ii}
is the same as equation (
C1
).
Then, the diagonal elements of
{J}({\Sigma_{t}})H_{t}^{\diamond}{J}({\Sigma_{t}})
are equal to zero.

Proof.

For (i),
the proof can be found in
Chen et al. 2025
.
For (ii), the proof can be found in
Chen et al. 2025
in the case of
3.2
. In the cases of
3.2
and
3.2
, substituting
J(\Sigma_{t})=\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}J_{n}
or
\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}J_{n}
, we have
\mathtt{Diag}(J_{n}H_{t}^{\diamond}J_{n})=0
.

\blacksquare

Denote
\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)=m_{N}^{\prime}(\theta)\Omega_{N}^{-1}m_{N}(\theta)
and
\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)=\bigl[\operatorname{E}\bigl(m_{N}(\theta)\bigl)\bigr]^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}\operatorname{E}\bigl(m_{N}(\theta)\bigl)
. Then we consider the following lemma to establish the consistency, i.e.,
\|\hat{\theta}_{gmm}-\theta_{0}\|=o_{p}(1)
. The idea is borrowed from
Su and Hoshino 2016
.

Lemma C3
.

Let
\Theta_{N}=\Gamma\times\Lambda_{N}
, where
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\pi}}
is a totally UB set with fixed dimension and
\Lambda_{N}=\{\lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{n}}:\|\lambda\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\lambda}\}
for each
l=1,\cdots,\ell_{n}
.
Under Assumptions
B1
-
B5
:

(i) For any
\theta\in\Theta_{N}
and
\eta>0
, if
\|\theta-\theta_{0}\|\geq\eta
, there exists a constant
C_{\eta}
such that

\bigl|\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta_{0})\bigr|\geq C_{\eta}
when
\ell_{n}^{1/2-\underline{\varsigma}}\to 0
as
(n,T)\to\infty
.

(ii)
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigl|\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}\bigl(\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)\bigr)\bigr|=o_{p}(1)
when
\ell_{n}^{2-\underline{\varsigma}}+\frac{\ell_{n}^{2}}{n}+\frac{\ell_{n}}{\sqrt{n(T-1)}}\to 0
as
(n,T)\to\infty
.

Proof.

(i) ensures the identification. We have

\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta_{0})=\varrho_{1N}(\theta)+2\varrho_{2N}(\theta),

where

\varrho_{1N}(\theta)=\left[\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\right]^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}\left[\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\right],

and

\varrho_{2N}(\theta)=\left[\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\right]^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0})).

By the mean-value theorem,
\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))=D_{N}(\tilde{\theta})(\theta-\theta_{0})
, where
D_{N}
is defined as equation (
C15
) and
\tilde{\theta}
, where
\tilde{\theta}=\theta_{0}+\kappa(\theta-\theta_{0})
with
|\kappa|<1
. Then,
\varrho_{1N}(\theta)=(\theta-\theta_{0})^{\prime}D_{N}(\tilde{\theta})^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}D_{N}(\tilde{\theta})(\theta-\theta_{0})
.
From Assumption
B5
(ii), we have
\|\Omega_{N}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}\leq\tfrac{1}{\mu_{\min}(\Omega_{N})}=O(1)
.
Combing Assumption
B5
(iii), we can find that there exists a constant
C_{\varrho}>0
, such that for all large
(n,T)
,
\varrho_{1N}(\theta)\geq C_{\varrho}\|\theta-\theta_{0}\|^{2}
. Hence, if
\|\theta-\theta_{0}\|\geq\eta
, then
\varrho_{1N}(\theta)\geq C_{\varrho}\eta^{2}
.
Note that

\begin{split}|\varrho_{2N}|&=\left|\left[\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\right]^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1/2}\Omega_{N}^{-1/2}\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\right|\leq|\varrho_{1N}|^{1/2}|\operatorname{E}^{\prime}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\Omega_{N}^{-1}\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))|^{1/2}\\
&\leq|\varrho_{1N}|^{1/2}\cdot\|\Omega_{N}^{-1/2}\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\|\end{split}

by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. From Lemma
S10
in the supplement and Assumption
B5
(ii), we have
\|\Omega_{N}^{-1/2}\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\|\leq\|\Omega_{N}^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}\|\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta_{0}))\|\leq C\ell_{n}^{1/2-\underline{\varsigma}}
, and thus

|\varrho_{2N}(\theta)|\leq C\ell_{n}^{1/2-\underline{\varsigma}}|\varrho_{1N}(\theta)|^{1/2}
. For
\|\theta-\theta_{0}\|\geq\eta
, we have

|\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta_{0})|\geq\varrho_{1N}(\theta)-|\varrho_{2N}(\theta)|\geq\varrho_{1N}(\theta)^{1/2}(\varrho_{1N}(\theta)^{1/2}-2C\ell_{n}^{1/2-\underline{\varsigma}}).

Since
\varrho_{1N}(\theta)^{1/2}\geq C_{\varrho}^{1/2}\eta^{1/2}
, for all large
(n,T)
, we can have
2C\ell_{n}^{1/2-\underline{\varsigma}}\leq C_{\varrho}^{1/2}\eta^{1/2}
as
\ell_{n}^{1/2-\underline{\varsigma}}
being sufficiently small, and thus

\bigl|\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta_{0})\bigr|\geq C_{\varrho}\eta=:C_{\eta}>0.

(ii) ensures the uniform convergence. We use the method proposed by
Andrews 1992
.
First, note that
\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)=H_{1N}(\theta)+2H_{2N}(\theta)
, where

H_{1N}(\theta)=\left[m_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))\right]^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}\left[m_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))\right],

and

H_{2N}(\theta)=\left[m_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))\right]^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta)).

From the Proof of Theorem
4.1
, we have

\|m_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))\|=O_{p}\Bigl(\ell_{n}^{3/2-\underline{\varsigma}}+\frac{\ell_{n}^{3/2}}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\ell_{n}}{n(T-1)}}\Bigr)
and
\operatorname{E}\|m_{N}(\theta)\|=O(\sqrt{\ell_{n}})
. Thus, using
\|\Omega_{N}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O(1)
, we have

\begin{split}|\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)|&\leq|H_{1N}(\theta)|+|H_{2N}(\theta)|\leq\|\Omega_{N}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}|\left(\|m_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))\|^{2}+2\|m_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}(m_{N}(\theta))\|\|m_{N}(\theta)\|\right)\\
&=O_{p}\Big(\ell_{n}^{2-\underline{\varsigma}}+\frac{\ell_{n}^{2}}{n}+\frac{\ell_{n}}{\sqrt{n(T-1)}}\Big)=o_{p}(1).\end{split}

Second, for any
\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in\Theta_{0}
, we have

\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\mathscr{G}_{N}(\tilde{\theta})=I_{1N}(\theta,\tilde{\theta})+2I_{2N}(\theta,\tilde{\theta})
, where

I_{1N}(\theta,\tilde{\theta})=\left[m_{N}(\theta)-m_{N}(\tilde{\theta})\right]^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}\left[m_{N}(\theta)-m_{N}(\tilde{\theta})\right],

and

I_{2N}(\theta,\tilde{\theta})=\left[m_{N}(\theta)-m_{N}(\tilde{\theta})\right]^{\prime}\Omega_{N}^{-1}m_{N}(\tilde{\theta}).

Then we have
m_{N}(\theta)-m_{N}(\tilde{\theta})=D_{N}(\bar{\theta})(\theta-\tilde{\theta})+o_{p}(1)
, where
\bar{\theta}=\tilde{\theta}+\kappa(\theta-\tilde{\theta})
with
|\kappa|<1
and
\|m_{N}(\tilde{\theta})\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=O_{p}(\sqrt{\ell_{n}})
.
Define
B_{e}=\{\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in\Theta_{0}:\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|\leq e\}
and
h_{e}=B_{e}\sup_{u\in\Theta_{0}}\|m_{N}(u)\|=B_{e}\cdot O_{p}(\sqrt{\ell_{n}})
.
Hence,

\begin{split}\sup_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in B_{e}}|I_{1N}(\theta,\tilde{\theta})|&\leq\sup_{u\in\Theta_{0}}\|D_{N}(u)\|_{\mathrm{sp}}^{2}\|\Omega_{N}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}B_{e}^{2}=O_{p}(B_{e}^{2})\\
\sup_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in B_{e}}|I_{2N}(\theta,\tilde{\theta})|&\leq\sup_{u\in\Theta_{0}}\|D_{N}(u)\|_{\mathrm{sp}}\|\Omega_{N}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}h_{e}=O(h_{e})=O_{p}(\sqrt{\ell_{n}}B_{e}).\end{split}

Then,
\sup_{\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in B_{e}}|\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\mathscr{G}_{N}(\tilde{\theta})|=O_{p}(B_{e}^{2}+h_{e})
, and we can choose
B_{e}=C\ell_{n}^{-1}
as
(n,T)\to\infty
such that
B_{e}+\sqrt{\ell_{n}}B_{e}\to 0
. Combining

|\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)-\operatorname{E}\mathscr{G}_{N}(\theta)|=o_{p}(1)
, the consistency follows.

\blacksquare

To derive the asymptotic normality, we consider the following lemma.

Lemma C4
.

Let
\mathscr{U}_{N}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n(T-1)}}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\prime}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\pi_{0},gmm}^{-1/2}K_{\pi}m_{\Delta}
, where the
\ell_{\pi}\times\ell_{m}
matrix

K_{\pi}
is defined in equation (
C21
),

\displaystyle m_{\Delta}\equiv m_{\Delta}(\theta_{0})=(\boldsymbol{E}_{N}^{*\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{N1}^{\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*},\cdots,\boldsymbol{E}_{N}^{*\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{N\ell_{p}}^{\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*},\boldsymbol{E}_{N}^{*\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{N})^{\prime},

(C2)

and

\boldsymbol{\tau}
is the
\ell_{\pi}\times 1
vector such that
\|\boldsymbol{\tau}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}=1
. Under Assumptions
B1
-
B4
, and
\sqrt{\frac{(T-1)\ell_{n}^{3}}{n}}+\sqrt{n(T-1)}\ell_{n}^{3/2-\underline{\varsigma}}\to 0
as
(n,T)\to\infty
,
\mathscr{U}_{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}N(0,1)
.

Proof.

By construction, we have
\operatorname{E}(\mathscr{U}_{N})=0
and
\operatorname{Var}(\mathscr{U}_{N})=1
.
Denote

\mathfrak{s}\equiv\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\prime}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\pi_{0},gmm}^{-1/2}K_{\pi}=(\mathfrak{s}_{1},...,\mathfrak{s}_{\ell_{p}},\underbrace{\mathfrak{s}_{\ell_{p}+1},...,\mathfrak{s}_{\ell_{m}}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{\ell_{q}}^{\prime}})

be then
1\times\ell_{m}
vector, then we have

\mathscr{U}_{N}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n(T-1)}}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{N}^{*\prime}(\sum_{l=1}^{\ell_{p}}\mathfrak{s}_{l}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{Nl}\mathbf{J}_{N})\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*}+\mathfrak{s}_{\ell_{q}}^{\prime}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n(T-1)}}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{N}^{*\prime}\mathscr{A}_{1N}\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*}+\mathscr{A}_{2N}\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*}\right)
,
where
\mathscr{A}_{1N}=\sum_{l=1}^{\ell_{p}}\mathfrak{s}_{l}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{Nl}\mathbf{J}_{N}
and
\mathscr{A}_{2N}=\mathfrak{s}_{\ell_{q}}^{\prime}\mathbf{Q}_{N}^{\prime}\mathbf{J}_{N}
.
Thus, we have
\|\mathscr{A}_{1N}\|_{1}=O(\sqrt{\ell_{n}})
,
\sum_{l=1}^{N}|\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}|=O_{p}(\sqrt{\ell_{n}})
.
This is because
\|\mathscr{A}_{1N}\|_{1}\leq\sum_{l=1}^{\ell_{p}}\|\mathfrak{s}_{l}\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{Nl}\mathbf{J}_{N}\|_{1}\leq\sum_{l=1}^{\ell_{p}}|\mathfrak{s}_{l}|\|\mathbf{J}_{N}\|_{1}^{2}\|P_{Nl}\|_{1}=O(\sqrt{\ell_{n}})
by Assumption
B4
, and

\sum_{l=1}^{N}|\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}|=O_{p}(\sqrt{\ell_{n}})
by an analogous argument.
Note that
\operatorname{E}(\boldsymbol{E}_{N}^{*\prime}\mathscr{A}_{1N}\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*})=\sum_{l=1}^{\ell_{p}}\mathfrak{s}_{l}\operatorname{E}\big(\boldsymbol{E}_{N}^{*\prime}(\mathbf{J}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{Nl}\mathbf{J}_{N})\mathbf{E}_{N}^{*}\big)=0
, from Lemma
S4
in the supplement, Assumption
1
,
2
, and
\sqrt{\frac{(T-1)\ell_{n}^{3}}{n}}+\sqrt{n(T-1)}\ell_{n}^{3/2-\underline{\varsigma}}\to 0
as
(n,T)\to\infty

we have

\mathscr{U}_{N}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n(T-1)}}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{l}+o_{p}(1)
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{l}=\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}\epsilon_{l}+2\epsilon_{l}\sum_{j=1}^{l-1}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\epsilon_{j}=\epsilon_{l}(\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}+2\sum_{j=1}^{l-1}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\epsilon_{j}).

(C3)

Consider the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}_{l}=\sigma(\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{l})
, where
\epsilon_{l}=\epsilon_{it}
, with
l=i+n(t-1)
and
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq t\leq T
. Then
\mathcal{F}_{l-1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{l}
and
\mathcal{X}_{l}
is
\mathcal{F}_{l}
-measurable
A4
A4

A4

We use the trivial
\sigma
-field to define
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
. Hence,
\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}|\mathcal{F}_{l-1})=0\ \text{a.s.}
, and
\{(\mathcal{X}_{l},\mathcal{F}_{l})|1\leq l\leq N\}
forms a martingale difference array. Then, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathcal{X}_{l}.

(C4)

From
Hall and Heyde 2014
, sufficient conditions
A5
A5

A5

We need to verify
\sum_{l=1}^{N}\operatorname{E}\left(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2}\mathbf{1}[|\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ}|>e]\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}0
for some constant
e
. By the elementary truncation bound
\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2}\mathbf{1}[|\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ}|>e]\leq e^{-\delta}|\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ}||^{2+\delta}
, and use the Markov inequality, we can verify the sufficient condition (
C5
).
to verify the martingale difference array CLT are

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{N}\operatorname{E}|\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ}|^{2+\delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}0

(C5)

for some
\delta>0
,
and

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{N}\left[\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2}|\mathcal{F}_{l-1})-\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2})\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}0.

(C6)

For (
C5
), denote
u=2+\delta
, we have
\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ}=N^{-u/2}\|\mathcal{X}_{l}\|_{1}^{u}
.
and
|\mathcal{X}_{l}|^{u}\leq C_{u}|\epsilon_{l}|^{u}|\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}|^{u}+2\|\mathscr{A}_{1N}\|_{1}^{u}\bigl(\max_{j<l}|\epsilon_{j}|^{u}\bigr)
by Minkowski inequality and the convex inequality that
(a+b)^{u}\leq C_{u}(a^{u}+b^{u})
, where
u>1
and
C_{u}=2^{u-1}
.
Thus,
\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}|\mathcal{F}_{l-1})\leq C_{\epsilon}^{2}C_{u}C\ell_{n}^{u/2}=O(\ell_{n}^{u/2})
and
\sum_{l=1}^{N}\operatorname{E}|\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ}|^{u}=O(N)\cdot O(N^{-u/2})\cdot O(\ell_{n}^{u/2})=O\Bigl(\frac{\ell_{n}^{2+\delta}}{(n(T-1))^{\delta}}\Bigr)=o(1)
according to Assumptions
1
,
B1
, and the rate conditions in Theorem
4.1
(ii).
A6
A6

A6

In Theorem
4.1
(ii), we need
\sqrt{\frac{(T-1)\ell_{n}^{3}}{n}}\to 0
which implies
\ell_{n}=o\big((\frac{n}{T})^{1/3}\big)
. Then,
O\bigl(\frac{\ell_{n}^{2+\delta}}{(n(T-1))^{\delta}}\bigr)=o\bigl(n^{(1-\delta)/3}T^{-(1+2\delta)/3}\bigr)
. Using
n=o(T^{p/2})
,
n^{(1-\delta)/3}T^{-(1+2\delta)/3}
goes to zero provided
(p/2)(1-\delta)<1+2\delta
. Therefore, if
0<\delta<1
, we need
\delta>\frac{p-2}{p+4}
.

For (
C6
), from (
C3
) and (
C4
), we have

\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2}|\mathcal{F}_{l-1})=N^{-1}\sigma_{l}^{2}(\operatorname{E}\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}+2\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\epsilon_{j})^{2}
, and

\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2})=N^{-1}\sigma_{l}^{2}(\operatorname{E}\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}^{2}+4\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2})
. Thus,

\begin{split}\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2}|\mathcal{F}_{l-1})-\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2})&=4N^{-1}\sigma_{l}^{2}\Big\{\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\epsilon_{j}+4\big[\big(\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\epsilon_{j}\big)^{2}-\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big]\Big\}\\
&=4N^{-1}\sigma_{l}^{2}\left[\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\epsilon_{j}+\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}(\epsilon_{j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2})+2\sum_{j<l}\sum_{k<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lk}\epsilon_{j}\epsilon_{k}\right]\end{split}

and

\sum_{l=1}^{N}\left[\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2}|\mathcal{F}_{l-1})-\operatorname{E}(\mathcal{X}_{l}^{\circ 2})\right]=4(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+2\Delta_{3}),

where

\begin{split}&\Delta_{1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\sigma_{l}^{2}\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\epsilon_{j},\Delta_{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\sigma_{l}^{2}\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}^{2}(\epsilon_{j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2})\ \text{and},\ \\
&\Delta_{3}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\sigma_{l}^{2}\sum_{j<k<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lk}\epsilon_{j}\epsilon_{k},\end{split}

and thus
\operatorname{E}(\Delta_{k})=0
for
k=1,2,3
.
For
\Delta_{1}
, rewrite by swapping sums:

\Delta_{1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\sigma_{l}^{2}\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}\sum_{j<l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\epsilon_{j}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}a_{j}\epsilon_{j}
. where
a_{j}=\sum_{l>j}\sigma_{l}^{2}\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}
.
Thus, and
\operatorname{E}(\Delta_{1})=\operatorname{Var}(\Delta_{1})\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon}^{2/(4+\delta_{\epsilon})}}{N^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}a_{j}^{2}
.
This is because
\operatorname{Var}(\epsilon_{j})\leq\operatorname{E}(\epsilon_{j}^{2})\leq(\operatorname{E}|\epsilon|^{4+\delta_{\epsilon}})^{2/(4+\delta_{\epsilon})}
by the norm inequality.
Using

|a_{j}|\leq C_{\epsilon}^{2/(4+\delta_{\epsilon})}\sum_{l>j}|\mathscr{A}_{2N,l}|\sum_{l>j}|\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}|=O_{p}(\ell_{n}),

and
\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}c_{j}^{2}=O_{p}(N\ell_{n}^{2})
, we have
\operatorname{Var}(\Delta_{1})=O\left(\frac{\ell_{n}^{2}}{N}\right)
and
\|\Delta_{1}\|=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell_{n}^{2}}{n(T-1)}}\right)
.
For
\Delta_{2}
, rewrite by swapping sums:

\Delta_{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}a_{2j}(\epsilon_{j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2})
, where
a_{2j}=\sum_{l>j}\sigma_{l}^{2}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}^{2}
. Note that

\operatorname{Var}(\epsilon_{j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2})=\operatorname{Var}(\epsilon_{j}^{2})\leq\operatorname{E}(\epsilon_{j}^{4})\leq C_{\epsilon}^{4/(4+\delta_{\epsilon})},

we have
\|\Delta_{2}\|=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell_{n}^{2}}{n(T-1)}}\right)
.
For
\Delta_{3}
, rewrite by swapping sums:

\Delta_{3}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq N-1}\epsilon_{j}\epsilon_{k}a_{3jk}
, where
a_{3jk}=\sum_{l>k}\sigma_{l}^{2}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lk}
. Hence,

\Delta_{3}^{2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{j<k}\sum_{r<s}a_{3jk}a_{3rs}\epsilon_{j}\epsilon_{k}\epsilon_{r}\epsilon_{s}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j<k}a_{3jk}^{2}\epsilon_{j}^{2}\epsilon_{k}^{2}+2\sum_{(j<k)<(r<s)}\epsilon_{j}\epsilon_{k}\epsilon_{r}\epsilon_{s}

where
(j<k)<(r<s)
means sum over distinct ordered pairs, so each cross term is counted once. If
\{j,k\}\cap\{r,s\}=\varnothing
or
j=r
but
k\neq s
, then
\operatorname{E}\epsilon_{j}\epsilon_{k}\epsilon_{r}\epsilon_{s}=0
. The only nonzero part is the paired case
(j,k)=(r,s)
with
j<k
and
r<s
. Hence,

\operatorname{E}(\Delta_{3}^{2})=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{j<k}a_{3ij}^{2}\operatorname{E}\epsilon_{i}^{2}\operatorname{E}\epsilon_{j}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon}^{4/(4+\delta_{\epsilon})}}{N^{2}}\sum_{j<k}a_{3ij}^{2}
.
By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

a_{3ij}^{2}=(\sum_{l>k}\sigma_{l}^{2}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lj}\mathscr{A}_{1N,lk})^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon}^{4/(4+\delta_{\epsilon})}(\sum_{l}b_{lj}^{2})(\sum_{l}b_{lk}^{2})\leq C_{\epsilon}^{4/(4+\delta_{\epsilon})}(\sup_{l}\sum_{l}|b_{lj}|)^{2}(\sup_{l}\sum_{l}|b_{lk}|)^{2}=O(\ell_{n}),

\operatorname{E}(\Delta_{3}^{2})=O(\frac{\ell_{n}^{2}}{N})
and thus
\|\Delta_{3}\|=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell_{n}^{2}}{n(T-1)}}\right)
. Combining Assumptions
1
,
B1
and
\sqrt{\frac{(T-1)\ell_{n}^{3}}{n}}\to 0
as
(n,T)\to\infty
, we have
\Delta_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
for
k=1,2,3
. The asymptotic normality of
\mathscr{U}_{N}
follows.

\blacksquare

We then establish the consistent estimator for
\Sigma_{t}
.

Lemma C5
.

Denote
\mathscr{W}_{t}(\theta)=J_{n}(V_{t}(\theta)-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{h=1}^{T}V_{h}(\theta))
for any
\theta\in\Theta
. We can estimate
\sigma_{i}^{2}
by
\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{\omega}_{it}^{2}
in the case of
3.2
, and estimate
\sigma_{t}^{2}
by
\hat{\sigma}_{t}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\omega}_{it}^{2}
in the case of
3.2
, where
\hat{\omega}_{it}=\omega_{it}(\hat{\theta})
, and
\omega_{it}=\omega_{it}(\theta_{0})
is the
i
-th entry of
\mathscr{W}_{t}
and
\hat{\theta}
is a consistent estimator of the true estimand
\theta_{0}
with
\|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\|=o_{p}(1)
.
Under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
B1
-
B5
, and as
(n,T)\to\infty
, the following hold:

(i) In the case of
3.2
,
|\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{i}^{2}|=o_{p}(1)
.

(ii) In the case of
3.2
,
|\hat{\sigma}_{t}^{2}-\sigma_{t}^{2}|=o_{p}(1)
.

Proof.

We define the centered components:

\Delta\epsilon_{it}=\epsilon_{it}-\bar{\epsilon}_{i\cdot}-\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot t}+\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot\cdot}
, where
\bar{\epsilon}_{i\cdot}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\epsilon_{it}
,
\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{it}
and

\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot\cdot}=\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i,t}\epsilon_{it}
. And
\Delta r_{it}=r_{it}-\bar{r}_{i\cdot}-\bar{r}_{\cdot t}+\bar{r}_{\cdot\cdot}
is defined by similar.
The proof of (ii) follows similarly to that of (i), so we will only provide the proof for (i) here.
Thus,
\omega_{it}=\Delta\epsilon_{it}+\Delta r_{it}
and
\hat{\omega}_{it}=\omega_{it}+(\hat{\omega}_{it}-\omega_{it})
. We have
\hat{\omega}_{it}^{2}=\omega_{it}^{2}+(\hat{\omega}_{it}-\omega_{it})^{2}+2\omega_{it}(\hat{\omega}_{it}-\omega_{it})
.
Thus, for (i),

\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{2}=\dfrac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\omega_{it}^{2}+\dfrac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\hat{\omega}_{it}-\omega_{it})^{2}+\dfrac{2}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\omega_{it}(\hat{\omega}_{it}-\omega_{it})=a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}.

For
a_{1}
, we have

a_{1}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\Delta\epsilon_{it})^{2}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\Delta r_{it})^{2}+\frac{2}{T}\Delta\epsilon_{it}\Delta r_{it}=a_{11}+a_{12}+a_{13}.

Note that
a_{11}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\epsilon_{it}^{2}+(\bar{\epsilon}_{i\cdot})^{2}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot t}^{2}+\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot\cdot}^{2}+\text{Cross Terms}
.
For the single square terms,
\operatorname{E}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\epsilon_{it}^{2}\right]=\sigma_{i}^{2}
,
\operatorname{E}[\bar{\epsilon}_{i\cdot}^{2}]=\operatorname{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\epsilon_{it}\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{T^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\operatorname{E}[\epsilon_{it}^{2}]+\frac{2}{T^{2}}\sum_{t<s}\operatorname{E}[\epsilon_{it}\epsilon_{is}]=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{T}=O(\frac{1}{T})
.
Similarly,
\operatorname{E}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot t}^{2}\right]=O(\frac{1}{n})
and
\operatorname{E}\left[\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot\cdot}^{2}\right]=O(\frac{1}{nT})
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the cross terms are bounded by the product of the magnitudes. For example, the cross term with the overall mean is:

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\epsilon_{it}\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot\cdot}=\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot\cdot}(\frac{1}{T}\sum\epsilon_{it})=\bar{\epsilon}_{\cdot\cdot}\cdot\bar{\epsilon}_{i\cdot}=O_{p}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}})\cdot O_{p}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}})=O_{p}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}T}).

Hence,
a_{1}=\sigma_{i}^{2}+o_{p}(1)
.
For
a_{12}
and
a_{13}
, we can show that
a_{12}=O_{p}(\ell_{n}^{-2\underline{\varsigma}})
and
|a_{13}|\leq 2\sqrt{a_{11}}\sqrt{a_{12}}=o_{p}(1)
.

Using the Mean Value Theorem, we have
\hat{\omega}_{it}-\omega_{it}=\frac{\partial\omega_{it}}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\big|_{\theta=\bar{\theta}}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})
. Thus,

a_{2}\leq\|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\|^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Big\|\frac{\partial\omega_{it}}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}\big|_{\theta=\bar{\theta}}\Big\|^{2}=o_{p}(1)\cdot O(1)=o_{p}(1)

by Assumption
B5
and the consistency of
\hat{\theta}
. We can show that
|a_{3}|=o_{p}(1)
By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Combining all above, we have
|\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{i}^{2}|=o_{p}(1)
.

\blacksquare

as

The proof can be analogous to the proof of Lemma
